@AGENTS.md

# Standing rules

- **A NEW INSTITUTION TYPE, LIKE A NEW ROLE, IS ONLY PROVEN BY SIGNING UP THROUGH THE REAL ONBOARDING SCREENS AS EACH OF ITS ROLES — NEVER BY FIXTURE OR DIRECT RPC CALL, HOWEVER THOROUGH.** PRD 5 built a real, verified clinic institution type across seven stages — real RLS, real RPCs, all four consent screens, a real director dashboard, a real caseload-verification RPC (`approve_staff_join()`'s own clinic branch) — and adversarially verified every one of those surfaces, repeatedly, with real signed-in sessions. None of it was ever reachable by a human, because the ONE screen a human uses to become a clinic user (`SchoolStaffRoleSelectContent.tsx`'s own role picker) had a placeholder in place of a clinic role picker for the entire seven stages, and nothing in that verification ever signed up through it — every fixture built its `institution_staff` row directly, every RPC was called as an already-signed-up test account. Found by Daniel's own first click, not by review, not by any adversarial check (see the dedicated entry under Deferred work for the full account). The general rule this earns, matching this file's own already-standing rule for a new ROLE ("prove a write policy with a real session doing the real operation," and the clinician-track specialty-gate finding before it): **every surface a signup flow LEADS TO can be perfectly correct and prove nothing about whether the signup flow itself LEADS ANYWHERE.** Fixtures and direct RPC calls skip the one step that actually gates a real person — the client-side entry point — every time, by construction; no amount of coverage on the far side of that gate substitutes for walking through it. When a new institution type, a new role, or any new self-service entry point ships, the FIRST verification step, before any RLS/RPC/consent/dashboard check, is: create a genuinely fresh account and go through the real screens, starting from the actual entry point (a code, a signup link, whatever a real person would use), not a shortcut into the middle of the flow.

- **THE FBA IS NOT TO BE TOUCHED. Daniel's own instruction, PRD 8 Stage 2, 19 Sept 2026.** It works, it is verified, it is the clinician track's centrepiece. No changes to its components, its sections, its rendering, its save paths, or its data shape — not for export polish, not for consistency, not as a side effect of anything else. This came up directly during Stage 2's own export build: the natural-looking move would have been to reuse `FbaSectionsReadOnly` (the FBA reader/print's own component) for the export screen's FBA rendering, and it was rejected specifically because that component's AFLS sub-section does its own live fetch against the current viewer's session (`useAflsAssessmentsForFba(fbaId)`) — reusing it for a director exporting a colleague's FBA would have meant either silently broken AFLS content, or extending `afls_assessments` with a new director-read branch nobody asked for, to make an unrelated screen's own borrowed component behave. The export screen was built with its own generic, purpose-built rendering instead (dumps `content_data` by key, excludes `afls` entirely) — see `clinicalExportRenderers.tsx`'s own header for the full reasoning. **If a future piece of work appears to need an FBA change, STOP and say so rather than making it. The answer is almost certainly to build around it, the way export did.**

- **AN EXPLICIT INSTRUCTION FROM DANIEL OVERRIDES ANY PRIOR PREFERENCE RECORDED IN A CODE COMMENT. Found live, 21 Sept 2026 — the diagnosis label mistake.** `diagnosisOptions.ts` had a code comment (written during the Stage 2 dedup, 15 Sept 2026) that declared "Autism" — not "Autism Spectrum Disorder" — the value to keep when collapsing a real duplicate ("Autism" vs "ASD (Autism Spectrum Disorder)"), on the reasoning that it was "the shorter, plainer of the two." Migration 0191 followed that comment. Daniel had asked, several times, for the surviving label to be "Autism Spectrum Disorder" — the fix did the opposite of his own repeated instruction, because the comment was read and followed as if it settled the question, when it was only ever one person's earlier opinion about which string to prefer. Fixed in 0278 (the correct label, the data remigrated, the comment rewritten so it no longer tells the next person to keep "Autism").

  **A code comment is a RECORD of a decision or a preference at the time it was written — it is not equal footing with an explicit instruction, and it does not survive contact with one that contradicts it.** A comment can be stale, can encode a guess later overridden verbally, or can simply be wrong about what was actually decided (this schema's own "READ THE LIVE DEFINITION, NOT THE FIRST ONE YOU FOUND" gotcha is the adjacent case — a comment/definition that predates a later, correct fix). When an explicit instruction and an in-repo comment disagree about what should happen, the instruction wins, always — and if a change is about to do the opposite of something Daniel has said, out loud, more than once, that is the moment to stop and say so, not to defer to whatever the code already claims.

- **A USER-REPORTED CAUSE IS A HYPOTHESIS, NOT EVIDENCE — EVEN WHEN THE USER FOUND IT THEMSELVES BY TRIAL AND ERROR.** 23 Sept 2026, a real claim-code report: a parent's redemption failed, and Daniel's own troubleshooting had landed on "it doesn't work until a clinician is connected" — a real correlation he'd observed firsthand. The first investigation read the code events (generate/revoke/regenerate/redeem timestamps) alone, concluded "ordinary mistyped code," and was pushed off that conclusion twice — once by a second round of questioning ("check what he actually reported"), which led to `clinician_access` timestamps that DID overlap the failure window, appearing to confirm his theory; a deliberate, controlled reproduction (fresh client, explicitly zero clinicians assigned, real parent session) then failed to reproduce anything, proving the mechanism doesn't depend on clinician assignment at all — the correlation was real, the causation wasn't. Daniel then confirmed directly: the code was simply entered wrong. **The very first read was correct the whole time; two further rounds of investigation happened because a person's own recollection of what they did was treated as settled fact rather than one candidate explanation to weigh against the data.** The general rule: when ruling out the simple, ordinary explanation (a typo, a stale value, a one-off) rests on someone's memory of their own troubleshooting rather than a log, a timestamp, or a reproducible test, say so explicitly and keep the simple explanation on the table — a plausible correlation a person noticed themselves is not proof of causation, and does not outrank it just because they're the one who lived through it. (`CHECK WWW`, kept from this episode: a parent claiming with zero clinician ever assigned had no standing coverage before this — genuinely worth having, independent of the false lead.)

- **AN ALLOWLIST ENTRY IS A CLAIM, NOT A FORMALITY — IT IS VERIFIED BY A REAL TEST BEFORE IT IS ADDED, AND CARRIES ITS REASON ALONGSIDE IT, THE SAME AS ANY OTHER CLAIM OF SAFETY IN THIS CODEBASE.** Found live, 22 Sept 2026, during the ABC role-misattribution fix (0277): `clinician-role-target-scan.mjs`'s own `update_clinician_last_review` allowlist entry claimed the trigger was "checked live this session; not a live bug" — it never had been. The entry silenced a real, live bug for as long as it stood, until Daniel asked for it to be answered through the real flow instead of trusted. Auditing that entry alone would have missed the actual size of the problem — every OTHER entry in both build-check allowlists (`clinic-copy-allowlist.json`'s 39, this scanner's own remaining 11 at the time) had been added the same unverified way, on the same session, by the same person, and none had been individually re-checked.

  **The full re-audit, done on Daniel's own instruction, checked two different things for every entry, not one:** (1) read the LIVE definition directly — the current source, not a remembered shape — and confirm the stated reason is still literally true; (2) remove the entry and re-run the scanner to confirm it is actually flagged without it, proving the entry does something rather than sitting there unexercised. Both checks matter, and they catch different failures: (1) alone would have missed two decorative entries (`reject_clinician`, `submit_clinician_verification`) that were never going to be flagged in the first place — neither function's live body contains the word "role" anywhere at all, so `TARGET_RE` never matched them, with or without an allowlist entry; the stated reason for each was true on its own terms, just answering a question the scanner was never asking. (2) alone would have missed a wrong stated reason that happened to reach a correct conclusion: `clinic-copy-allowlist.json`'s own `passport/dashboard/page.tsx:1120` entry claimed a string was "PassportIncidentsSection's own heading, only ever rendered inside a gated conditional" — the string is actually inside a plain code COMMENT, never rendered to any user in any state at all, a stronger guarantee than the one claimed, but not the one written down. Every other entry across both allowlists (37 of 39 copy entries, 10 of 12 role-target entries) passed both checks and is now recorded against the specific live migration, table, or component it was verified from — see each scanner's own file for the corrected set.

  **The general rule this earns, generalizing past this one incident:** before adding a name/line to an allowlist (or trusting one already there), do the same two things — read the live definition/source directly and confirm the claim, and confirm the check actually flags the thing without the entry. A reason that sounds right, or that would have been right at some earlier point, is not the same as a reason confirmed right now against the current code. An unverified allowlist entry is not a neutral placeholder; it is a standing claim that something is safe, made on nobody's authority, and it will be trusted by name the next time this check runs — exactly the shape that let one specific mistake become a live production bug.

# Supabase / Postgres gotchas

Learned the hard way, on the School Incident Log build. Read before writing any code that touches RLS.

- **AN UNORDERED `SELECT ... INTO` AGAINST `institution_staff` IS SAFE ONLY WHERE A CONSTRAINT STRUCTURALLY CAPS THE RESULT AT ONE ROW — NOWHERE ELSE, AND POSTGRES WILL NEVER WARN YOU.** `SELECT INTO` never errors on multiple matching rows, `STRICT` variants aside — it silently takes whichever row the planner happens to read first. `institution_staff_one_active_per_institution` (0100) is a partial unique index on `(institution_id, user_id) where deactivated_at is null and rejected_at is null` — it caps the result to one row ONLY when a query is scoped to (a) one specific `institution_id` AND (b) `deactivated_at is null` (or the stronger `approved_at is not null`, which implies it via the schema's own not-both-approved-and-rejected check). Drop either half — scan across every institution the caller happens to be staff at, or omit the standing filter — and the result is arbitrary, compiles cleanly, runs cleanly, and returns a wrong answer nobody notices until a caller happens to hold a second qualifying row.

  **Four real instances, every one found by accident while doing something unrelated, never by deliberately auditing for this shape:**
  - `derive_countersign_fields()` — fixed in `0103` (`order by s.created_at desc limit 1`, closing the "old deactivated row vs. new active row, same institution" case), silently dropped by `0245`'s own from-scratch rewrite, restored in `0256`. This file's own "A REBUILD CAN SILENTLY DROP A GATE" entry covers this instance in full.
  - `create_bsp()` — no institution scoping at all (an unordered scan of every clinic the caller is staff at); found building PRD 8 Stage 3's own verification fixture when a multi-clinic practitioner's BSP was silently attributed to the wrong clinic.
  - `hand_over_principal()` — the deepest of the four: the function took no `institution_id` parameter at all, so the ambiguous lookup wasn't an implementation slip inside an otherwise-correct function, it was the function's ENTIRE mechanism for knowing which institution it was acting on. Filtering the lookup alone could not have fixed this — the caller has to STATE the institution, not have it inferred.
  - `send_message()` — institution-scoped, but missing the `deactivated_at`/`approved_at` filter every OTHER institution-scoped lookup in this schema already carries, so the same "rejoin" case `0103` closed for countersigning was open here too — and the picked-up role isn't just an authorization gate, it's written permanently into `messages.sender_role` as attribution.

  All three live instances (the fourth, `derive_countersign_fields()`, already had its own fix) closed together in `0261`, each with the shape that actually fits it — `create_bsp()` now derives its institution from `clinician_access.engaged_by_institution_id` for the specific child being acted on (a real `unique(passport_id, clinician_id)` constraint, so structurally at most one row) rather than from the caller's general membership; `hand_over_principal()` gained an explicit `p_institution_id` parameter, stated by the caller (who always knows which institution they're acting in) rather than inferred; `send_message()` gained the missing standing filter, matching the pattern everywhere else.

  **The one that LOOKED identical and wasn't, checked properly rather than assumed — the right shape of proof for a "ruled safe" claim**: `create_incident_stamp()`'s own caller-role lookup is institution-scoped with the correct `deactivated_at is null and approved_at is not null` filter — structurally, provably capped at one row by the same partial unique index, confirmed by reading the index definition directly rather than trusting that the query "looks the same as the others." Saying why a pattern-alike is safe is worth more than saying "checked" — the reasoning is what lets the next reader tell the two apart without re-deriving it.

  **A dual-membership adversarial check (CHECK TTT, `scripts/incident-log-test/adversarial-verify.mjs`) proves each of the three fixes against a real caller holding a second qualifying row** — a clinician on staff at two clinics engaged for a child at only one of them; one person active and approved as principal at two institutions simultaneously, handed over both directions on the same person; a rejoined staff member with an old deactivated row and a new active row of a different role at the same institution. This is enumerated regression protection for the four now-known instances, not a general detector — catching a FIFTH, not-yet-found instance of this shape needs either a static scan of migration SQL for the pattern (heuristic, real false-positive/false-negative risk — `create_incident_stamp()`'s own text looks identical to the unsafe versions and isn't) or noticing it by hand, the way all four so far were found. Worth being honest about that limit rather than implying the check is broader than it is.

  **`hand_over_principal()`'s own signature change meant every EXISTING caller in this suite needed updating too, not just the new check** — found immediately, not later: CHECK X (the function's own dedicated coverage, 0102) and CHECK CCC (a real-handover regression control) both called it with the old 4-param shape across thirteen call sites total, which would have failed outright the moment the new required `p_institution_id` was missing. Fixed in the same pass, each site given the correct institution id already in scope at that point in its own fixture. Also surfaced, and fixed before the gate run, not glossed over: two earlier attempts at a full run crashed on this exact mismatch and left real orphans behind — `principal_handovers`/`institution_staff`/`passports` under two stray `INCVERIFY####XL/XS/XO` institutions, and eleven `handoverx.*@thebehaviourhive.com` accounts (CHECK X's own fixture uses fixed, non-randomised emails, unlike `CODE`'s own per-run random suffix, so a crash there permanently blocks every later run's user creation until cleaned by hand). Confirmed removed before the clean run, confirmed zero orphans again after it.

  **VERIFIED — full adversarial suite, real signed-in sessions throughout, 1173/1173, zero failures, rate limit raised and reverted per the standing gate rule.** CHECK TTT (the new dual-membership check) 10/10; CHECK X (`hand_over_principal()`'s own dedicated coverage, all thirteen call sites now on the new signature) 43/43; CHECK CCC (a real handover as a regression control for an unrelated RPC) 21/21 — both proving the signature change didn't just compile, it holds under every guard the suite already had for this function. Fixture torn down by the suite's own end-of-block cleanup in every case; checked directly afterward for both the fixed-email pattern (`handoverx.`) and the check's own institution-code pattern (`INCVERIFY`, `TTT`) — zero orphans.

- **RLS on UPDATE silently filters, it doesn't error.** A `.update()` whose target row fails the policy's USING clause returns `{ data: [], error: null }` — no thrown error, no signal at all. Never trust the absence of a client-visible error as proof a write succeeded, or that a negative test correctly blocked something. Re-read the actual persisted state afterward via a privileged (service-role) query.

- **A STALE SNAPSHOT LOOKS EXACTLY LIKE A FAILED WRITE.** `SignOffCard` fetched `get_incident_signoff_summary()` once on mount, keyed on `incidentId`. A teacher answered a question, the write succeeded and persisted, and the card kept reporting "not recorded" from before the answer. Diagnosed twice as a write bug — once correctly for a different cause, once wrongly. When a summary or derived view disagrees with the database, check WHEN it was read before checking whether the write landed.

  **Second confirmed instance, same session, worse presentation:** `CountersignCard`'s own `summary` had the identical shape — fetched once, keyed on `[incidentId]` alone, no refresh signal from its own successful action. A real countersign wrote `countersigned_at`/`countersigned_by` correctly (confirmed directly against the database), the sheet closed, and the card kept rendering "Countersign record" — not a stale VALUE this time, a stale ABSENCE OF ANY VISIBLE CHANGE AT ALL, which reads as "nothing happened" far more convincingly than a stale field ever could. Reported live as "no confirmation appeared" — the confirmation genuinely had appeared and the action had genuinely succeeded; the screen afterward just looked identical to before, with nothing to signal otherwise. `SignOffCard`'s own equivalent bug never surfaced this way only because `isLocked` flips true on success and unmounts the whole component — a coincidence of that specific card's gating, not a property of the fix. `AddAmendmentSheet`'s own `onAdded` callback, three lines below the bug in the exact same file, already had the correct fix (`applyCountersignData(await fetchCountersignData())`) for a different action on the same component — the working pattern was present and simply wasn't applied to the action that needed it most. When any card owns its own derived summary AND its own mutating action, the action's own success handler must refresh that SAME local state directly — never assume a parent's refetch (`reloadKey`, a prop bump) will reach a component that isn't unmounted/remounted by it.

  **AND IT WAS DISMISSED ONCE AS A TOOLING QUIRK.** The CountersignCard instance was seen during live verification, attributed to a browser click not landing, and closed. The action had succeeded and the card rendered unchanged. When a live check produces "the click did not seem to work" but the database shows the write landed, that is a stale read, not a tooling problem — check the component's own refetch before blaming the browser.

- **A WRITE BEFORE A RAISE DOES NOT SURVIVE.** An uncaught exception rolls back the whole transaction the RPC runs in, including any insert or update earlier in the same function. `redeem_passport_claim_code()` recorded a rate-limit failure and then raised — so the limiter recorded nothing at all from the day it shipped. If a function needs to record something AND refuse, it must return rather than raise, or record in a separate transaction. This is not obvious from reading the function — it reads as though the insert lands and then the caller errors. Found by a targeted one-sign-in diagnostic (12 wrong-code attempts, `code_lookup_attempts` stayed at 0 rows every time), not by code review; fixed in migration 0116 by matching `lookup_passport_by_code()`'s own original pattern (0034) — `insert ...; return;` for the case that needs to record and fail, a normal zero-row return instead of an exception.

  The rule alone over-triggers — a schema-wide audit immediately after this fix found three OTHER functions with the identical textual shape (write, then later a raise) that are correctly written, not the same bug: `send_message()`, `create_incident_stamp()`, and `generate_passport_claim_code()`. In each, the write is part of the entity being atomically constructed — a message, an incident stamp, a code reissue — and rolling it back on failure (no valid recipients, an unlinked child, ten random-code collisions) is the correct behavior, not a silent loss. The actual bug class is narrower: a write to a table whose entire JOB is to survive the call's own failure — an audit log, an attempt counter, a rate limiter. `code_lookup_attempts` is currently the only such table in this schema. Audit any function that writes then raises against that distinction specifically, not the bare textual pattern — without it, the next audit produces false alarms and someone "fixes" correct atomicity.

- **Chaining `.select()` onto `.insert()` requires the new row to pass the table's SELECT policy WITHIN THE SAME STATEMENT** (PostgREST sends `Prefer: return=representation`). If a row's visibility depends on related rows that don't exist yet in that same transaction — e.g. an incident whose visibility partly depends on its children, inserted a moment later — the chained `.select()` can fail even though the bare insert, and a separate follow-up `.select()` afterward, both succeed. Don't work around this with a client-generated id (`crypto.randomUUID()`) to dodge the RETURNING clause; if the row's own existence should never be structurally possible without its children, fix it properly with a single atomic SECURITY DEFINER RPC that inserts everything in one transaction.

- **`.upsert()` TRIGGERS THE SELECT POLICY IMPLICITLY.** supabase-js sends `Prefer: return=representation` on `.upsert()` by default even with no `.select()` chained, so the new row must pass the SELECT policy within the same statement. Gotcha #3 above describes this for `.select()` chained onto `.insert()`; `.upsert()` does it invisibly, with nothing in the source to make it obvious. 0117 rewrote `passports`' SELECT policy to `owns_passport()` and silently broke every first-time passport creation, because the guardian row a trigger creates after the insert is not visible to the representation check in the same statement — live in production from the moment 0117 shipped until PRD 3 Stage 1 found it by accident, debugging an unrelated check. Not a claimed-guardian edge case: every brand-new parent's very first save, on both `/passport/welcome` and `/passport/section-a`. When changing ANY table's SELECT policy, grep for `.upsert()` against that table.

- **A POLICY THAT LOOKS UP SOMEONE ELSE RUNS UNDER THE CALLER'S OWN RLS.** A subquery inside one table's own RLS policy is not privileged — it executes under the CALLING session's own permissions, exactly like a query the client ran directly. If that subquery reads a row belonging to someone OTHER than the caller, on a table whose own SELECT policy is self-only, it silently finds nothing — not an error, not a warning, just an `EXISTS` that's always false for the one case that matters. `clinical_lead_scope`'s policies (`0207`, PRD 5 Stage 2) both did this against `institution_staff`, whose own SELECT policy has been self-only (`auth.uid() = user_id`) since `0009`: the WRITE policy looked up the LEAD being scoped (not the director doing the scoping) to authorize the director's own insert; the READ policy looked up the same lead's row to decide whether a COLLEAGUE could see it. Both silently failed for their only real use case — a director scoping someone who isn't themselves, a colleague viewing someone else's scope — and neither ever threw an error to say so. **It survived because the one case that DOES work — a lookup that happens to resolve to the caller's own row — is the one nobody needs**, and because Stage 2's own verification exercised the RPCs and toggles built that same stage, never a raw client-side write against `clinical_lead_scope` itself — a table with no RPC in front of it at all, direct RLS the only path. Found live in PRD 5 Stage 4 (a real director's real INSERT, refused with a bare RLS violation on data that was genuinely valid), fixed in `0217` with two `SECURITY DEFINER` helpers — the same pattern `institution_staff_has_current_standing()` already established for this exact problem, applied to a case nobody had needed it for yet. A deliberate sweep afterward, not just the fix, found the second (read-policy) instance — it would never have surfaced from fixing the write policy alone, since it's a different policy on the same table with the identical shape. Swept `institution_staff` and `passport_guardians` (this schema's only two self-only-SELECT tables) across all ~90 policy blocks referencing either; no third instance found. **The general rule: when a policy's own USING or WITH CHECK needs to authorize based on a row belonging to someone other than the caller, on a table with a restrictive SELECT policy, that lookup needs a `SECURITY DEFINER` helper — a raw `EXISTS` will compile, run, and silently find nothing.**

**THE SAME SHAPE, A THIRD TABLE, CAUGHT BEFORE SHIPPING — Tier 1 clinic UI, item 2, 21 Sept 2026.** Not `institution_staff` or `passport_guardians` this time, and not a policy's own internal subquery — a raw CLIENT-SIDE query, against `passport_institution_links`, hitting the identical structural failure. Building the clinic Incidents tab's own visibility rule ("hide for a clinic-only child, show for one who also attends a school") needed to answer "does this passport link to a school anywhere" — and the first draft answered it with a plain `.from("passport_institution_links").select("institutions(type)").eq("passport_id", ...)` from the clinic director's own session. `passport_institution_links`' own "Teachers can view links for their institution" policy (`0014`) is scoped to `s.institution_id = passport_institution_links.institution_id` — the CALLER'S OWN institution only, not self-only exactly (a different shape from the two tables already swept above) but the identical consequence: a clinic director querying that table directly for a shared child would only ever see the clinic's own link row, never the school's — a different `institution_id` entirely, invisible across the boundary, silently. The query would have compiled, run, returned one row, and `hasSchoolLink` would have resolved to `false` permanently, for every dual-attendee child, forever — hiding real school-incident access a director is meant to have. Caught by re-reading the actual policy text before treating the client query as trustworthy, not found live by a fixture this time — fixed with `get_passport_has_school_link()` (`0265`), a real `SECURITY DEFINER` boolean check, before this ever reached a real account. **The general rule holds beyond RLS policies written in SQL: a raw client-side query is just as exposed to this failure as a policy's own internal subquery — "does X exist that the caller isn't otherwise scoped to see" always needs a `SECURITY DEFINER` check, whichever side of the client/policy boundary the lookup is written on.**

- **`strategy_bank`/`bank_assets` WERE WRITABLE BY A SCHOOL, NOT JUST READABLE BY ONE — A REAL INTEGRITY HOLE ON A SHARED CLINICAL LIBRARY, FOUND 21 Sept 2026 WHILE BUILDING THE DIRECTOR/LEAD CLINICAL-WORK GATE, DISTINCT FROM THE COSMETIC NAV-LINK ISSUE FOUND ALONGSIDE IT.** `_is_director_of_institution()` and `_is_verified_clinician_at_institution()` (`0238`, the strategy bank's own two authorization helpers) checked ROLE ONLY — `role = 'principal'` and `role = 'clinician'` respectively, at the caller's own institution — with no institution-TYPE check at all. Every real school in this schema can hold a `role = 'clinician'` `institution_staff` row (school-engaged clinicians are a long-standing, real pattern — `session_notes`/`assessments` have supported it for months), which means a school's own principal already satisfied `_is_director_of_institution()` for their own school, and a school-engaged clinician already satisfied `_is_verified_clinician_at_institution()` the same way — both **before** the client-side gate on `/clinician/strategy-bank` was ever reachable by a school principal at all (that gate was `useRequireRole(["clinician", "principal"])`, no institution-type check either, closed in the same pass).

  **Why this is a genuine write-authorization gap, not merely a display leak.** `strategy_bank`'s own UPDATE policy (curate/retire, `is_active` toggle and content edits) is gated on `_is_director_of_institution()` alone, with no additional check that the target row's own `institution_id` belongs to a clinic. Nothing about the UPDATE policy's own shape cared whether the institution attached to that row was a school or a clinic — only whether the caller was a currently-standing principal AT that institution. A school with its own school-engaged clinician (who could freely INSERT into `strategy_bank`/`bank_assets` scoped to that school's own `institution_id`, via the identically type-blind INSERT check) would have produced real rows a school principal could then curate or retire through a policy that was never supposed to admit them at all. This is not "a school sees a clinic's data" (cross-institution isolation was never actually broken — every check was still correctly scoped to the CALLER's own `institution_id`) — it is "a school can write into and administer a table whose entire purpose (PRD 7's own opening argument: 'the clinic's own accumulated, refined library') presupposes it belongs to a clinic," because nothing in the schema ever encoded that the table is clinic-only.

  **Fixed in `0267`** — both `_is_director_of_institution(p_institution_id)` and `_is_verified_clinician_at_institution(p_institution_id)` now additionally join `institutions` and require `inst.type = 'clinic'`, closing the gap at the authorization-helper layer, not just the client page that happens to call it today. `_is_director_of_institution()` is also called from `cross_organisation_grants` (`0245`), always on a GRANTING institution — already independently guaranteed `type = 'clinic'` by that migration's own direction trigger — so the added check is a genuine no-op there and a real fix here, confirmed by reading the trigger directly before assuming it, not asserted from the function's name.

  **The general rule this earns, alongside — not instead of — the caller-scoped-lookup family already documented above:** a `SECURITY DEFINER` helper that authorizes "is this caller a director/clinician at this institution" is not automatically scoped to the KIND of institution a feature is built for, unless it says so explicitly. Every one of this schema's clinic-only tables needs its own authorization helpers to independently re-check institution type, the same way `is_verified_clinic_director_or_lead()` (`0266`) does for the clinician-track gate itself — trusting "nothing today produces a row that would satisfy this for the wrong institution type" is exactly the load-bearing assumption that made this gap invisible until someone deliberately went looking for every place a school could reach a clinic-only surface.

- **FOR ANY NEW TABLE WITH ITS OWN WRITE POLICY, PROVE THE POLICY WITH A REAL SESSION DOING THE REAL OPERATION.** The verification lesson behind the entry above, stated as its own rule because it generalizes past this one table. `0207`'s own Stage 2 verification proved every RPC it shipped and every toggle it added — real sessions, real refusals, real positive controls — but never once tried a raw client-side `INSERT` against `clinical_lead_scope` itself, because nothing in that stage's own scope needed to write a real scope row yet. The bug sat live, unexercised, until Stage 4 needed the table for real and the first genuine attempt hit it. Testing the RPC that happens to sit ABOVE a table, or writing the row via the service role to set up a fixture, both bypass RLS entirely and prove nothing about whether the policy itself works — this schema has `CLAUDE.md` entries for both of those separately, and this is the same family: a policy's own claim ("director-only," "any staff can view this") is only proven by a real, unprivileged session actually attempting the real write or read the policy is supposed to gate.

- **`CREATE OR REPLACE FUNCTION` DOES NOT COLLAPSE A LONGER PARAMETER LIST ONTO A SHORTER ONE — IT CREATES A SECOND, SILENT OVERLOAD.** Adding a new trailing parameter (even with a default) via bare `CREATE OR REPLACE` does not replace the existing function; Postgres keeps both signatures as independent overloads. `send_message()` did this twice — 0062 added `p_abc_log_id`/`p_strategy_update` without dropping 0061's 5-param original, and 0168 (staff-to-staff messaging) repeated the identical mistake adding `p_institution_id`, explicitly citing 0161 as a safe precedent for the pattern. That precedent was never actually safe — it just had never been tested by a caller that omits the newest parameter, because every real client call site in this codebase has always passed every parameter explicitly. It surfaced immediately the first time CHECK NNN called `send_message` with a narrower, realistic parameter set: `Could not choose the best candidate function between: send_message(...7 params), send_message(...8 params)`. Fixed in 0169 by dropping every historical signature explicitly, then creating the function fresh, exactly once — the same shape this codebase already gets right everywhere else: `get_institution_incidents` (0166), `get_institution_staff_roster` (0097, then again 0100), and `revoke_passport_claim_code` (0126) each grew a parameter at some point, and every one of them correctly did `drop function if exists ...(old signature); create function ...` first, confirmed by reading each one directly, not assumed. `send_message` was the only function in the schema that skipped that step. **The actual rule, corrected: a new trailing defaulted parameter is NEVER safe to add via bare `CREATE OR REPLACE` — always `DROP FUNCTION IF EXISTS` for every prior signature first, then `CREATE FUNCTION`, with no exception for "it's just one optional param."** When touching any function whose parameter list has grown before, grep its own migration history first (same function name, differing parameter counts) rather than trusting the most recent comment's own claim that CREATE OR REPLACE was sufficient — that claim itself can be wrong and uncaught, exactly as it was here.

- **A REBUILD CAN SILENTLY DROP A GATE AND CLAIM IT DID NOT — TWO CONFIRMED INSTANCES, SAME ROOT CAUSE.** `0060` replaced `fba_afls_data` with `afls_assessments` and its own header comment says "same gate `fba_afls_data` always used". True for the parent-read policy, false for all three clinician write policies — the completed-FBA lock was lost. It went unnoticed for months and a real post-lock write occurred (see the dedicated Stage 8 entry under "Deferred work" for the full incident). When a migration replaces a table, diff the OLD table's policies against the new ones explicitly rather than trusting the migration's own account of itself.

  **Second instance, this session, self-inflicted: `0245` (PRD 8 Stage 1, incident withholding) rewrote `derive_countersign_fields()` from scratch to add the withhold-decision logic, and silently reverted `0103`'s own already-shipped fix for a real multi-row role-lookup ambiguity** — `0103`'s own header describes the exact symptom (a freshly-promoted principal's first countersign attributed `countersigned_via='grant', countersigned_role_at_time='class_teacher'`, their OLD closed row's role, not their new active one) and `0245` reproduced it word-for-word, found by `CHECK X`'s own coverage during PRD 9 Stage 1's full-suite gate run. Fixed in `0256`, restoring `0103`'s filter (`deactivated_at is null and approved_at is not null`) plus `order by created_at desc limit 1` into `0245`'s current body — nothing about `0245`'s own withhold logic changed. Confirmed as the ONLY affected assertion in the suite before fixing, not assumed: every other check touching `countersigned_role_at_time`/`countersigned_via` was read directly, and each one's countersigning caller holds exactly one relevant `institution_staff` row at the moment of their own call (a single-role principal, a single-role class_teacher grant-holder, or — `CHECK V` — a value frozen BEFORE a later deactivation creates a second row) — `CHECK X`'s handover successor is the only caller in the whole suite with two rows at the moment of their own countersign.

  **Both instances share the identical root cause, and it is narrower than "diff the old policies" — it's specifically about REWRITING rather than READING.** `0245` did read the live definition of `derive_countersign_fields()` before touching it — this is not a violation of "read the live definition, not the first one you find" (that rule is already satisfied here). It read the wrong ONE: a version of the function that predated `0103`'s fix, without checking whether any migration between that version and now had changed it for a reason still worth preserving. **The mechanical check that would have caught both instances: before rewriting a function from scratch (as opposed to extending its current live body in place), grep every migration for that function's name, read every hit in order, and confirm each earlier fix survives whatever the rewrite is about to replace it with.** A function's own git-style history is not visible from reading its current definition alone — a rewrite that starts from an old remembered shape, or from a version copied out of an early migration for reference, can silently un-fix something a later migration deliberately changed, and nothing about the rewrite compiling or running will say so.

- **A TYPESCRIPT INTERFACE NARROWER THAN THE RPC IT TYPES IS INVISIBLE — THE DATA READS AS ABSENT, NOT UNDECLARED.** `build_staff_attestations_summary()` has returned `withdrawn_at`/`withdrawal_reason` for every staff row since 0149, regardless of current status — a real, working, already-shipped signal. `SignOffCard.tsx`'s own `StaffAttestation` interface only ever declared `incident_staff_id`/`name`/`has_account`/`status`/`status_label`/`blocks_signoff`. The two fields sat in every API response, unused, for weeks — not because the data was missing, but because nothing in the type declared it, so nothing in the component ever looked. Found while building the sign-off confirm-sheet rework, not by anyone deliberately auditing this gap. When a client interface types an RPC's `jsonb`/`json` return, treat a narrower interface as a standing question — check the RPC's own `jsonb_build_object` (or equivalent) for fields the interface dropped — rather than assuming "not in the type" means "not in the response."

- **A FIXTURE THAT REUSES AN ACCOUNT TESTS THE SECOND CALL, NOT THE FIRST.** When a policy or check depends on a row a trigger creates in the same statement, the first-ever invocation is a different code path — visibility depends on something that does not exist yet. 0117 rewrote `passports`' SELECT policy to `owns_passport()` and broke every first-time passport creation for weeks, invisibly, because every fixture already had a passport row. When a policy references a trigger-populated row, that function's first invocation needs its own dedicated fixture. Not a blanket instruction to use fresh accounts — the precondition is the trigger dependency.

- **HOME LOGS REACH THE CLASSROOM BY DESIGN, VIA THE CLINICIAN.** A parent's home ABC entry is invisible to school staff. A clinician with active access reads the full home history, and `approve_fba_strategies()` publishes what they choose into `passport_clinical_content` as `strategy_school` / `strategy_shared` / `trigger` / `setting_event` — all school-visible. `strategy_home` is deliberately excluded. The raw log never crosses; the clinical interpretation does. This is shipped and working. Do not treat the home/school boundary as a dead end and build a route around it.

- **An embedded PostgREST join across a table with its own RLS returns EMPTY on the joined side, not an error.** `.select("incident_children(child_index, passports(child_name))")` silently drops the `passports` fields if the caller's session doesn't satisfy `passports`' own SELECT policy — even when the outer row (`incident_children`) is fully visible. The page renders wrong data (e.g. a name silently becomes "Unnamed child") with nothing failing anywhere to notice. When a relationship is deliberately broader than the joined table's own RLS — e.g. institution-roster-wide visibility that doesn't require the ordinary per-user grant (`passport_access`) — resolve it through a dedicated SECURITY DEFINER RPC instead of an embedded join. In this codebase: roster-scoped child names always resolve through `get_institution_child_roster()`, never a direct or embedded `passports(...)` read.

- **Whenever a migration changes behavior that existing adversarial checks depend on, re-audit those checks in the same session — and say so in the report.** A check can quietly become a no-op instead of failing: e.g. `get_parent_incidents()`'s gate changed from `status <> 'draft'` to `teacher_signed_at is not null`, which correctly made an existing check's row count go from 1 to 0 — but a *second* assertion right next to it read `row = rows?.[0]` and asserted a property was absent on that (now-undefined) row, which is vacuously true whether or not the property would actually leak. Nothing failed, so nothing flagged it; it was only caught by deliberately grepping for every place the changed function/table/column was referenced and reading each call site against the new behavior. Do that grep as a standing step after any migration that changes an existing function's gate, a table's shape, or a trigger's condition — not just when something conveniently fails first.

- **WHEN A FUNCTION'S RETURN CONTRACT CHANGES, UPDATE ITS CALLERS IN THE SAME PASS.** `0113` changed `get_child_clinical_document_status()` to always return one row with an `is_authorized` flag, instead of zero rows for "no document yet." Its own comment warned future callers to check `is_authorized` explicitly; the two EXISTING callers (`ClinicalSupportSection.tsx`, `CalmUnlockSheet.tsx`) were never updated, and every FBA-less passport showed "FBA complete" linking to `/passport/fba/null` — live from the moment 0113 shipped until Stage 5 Step 3 found it by chance, walking a genuinely fresh passport end-to-end. A comment protects readers, not code. Changing a `RETURNS TABLE` shape means grepping every consumer in the same migration, not just documenting the new contract for whoever writes the next one.

- **READ THE LIVE DEFINITION, NOT THE FIRST ONE YOU FIND.** A policy or function may have been altered several times. `0069`'s incident-edit policy genuinely lacked a `deactivated_at` check; `0105`/`0106`/`0107` superseded it and the current definition is correct. Flagging the historical version as a live gap wastes a fix and, worse, could produce one that reverts a later correction. Always resolve to the highest-numbered definition before concluding anything.

- **NINE INDEPENDENT LINEAGES CHECK `institution_staff` MEMBERSHIP, plus ~20 hand-written raw checks in individual RPCs.** The correct ones are correct by accumulated diligence, not by any enforced mechanism — `institution_staff_has_current_standing()` (0105) exists for exactly this and was used in one place until `0119`. Any NEW code checking `institution_staff` membership calls that helper. Never hand-write the `deactivated_at`/`approved_at` conditions again — it has been got wrong three times, including twice on the same policy across two dedicated fix passes (`passport_institution_links`' teacher-view policy, 0035 and 0112, neither of which was reading for this).

- **A COLUMN NAME IS A CLAIM. IF NOTHING WRITES IT TRUE FOR THE REASON ITS NAME SAYS, THE CLAIM IS FALSE.** `passport_institution_links.approved_by_parent` was a genuine consent record when it had exactly one writer: a parent, approving a school by code. PRD 3 Stage 2 removed that writer (`ShareBottomSheet`'s `handleApprove()`) as part of the school-led pivot, but `create_school_passport()` still sets it `true` on every school-created child — correctly, since a school creating its own child's record has no parent to seek approval from, and the flag is what keeps that link visible to gates written when it meant something else. The column is now a COMPATIBILITY DEFAULT, not a consent record, for every row it produces. Left un-commented, `approved_by_parent = true` on a school-created passport reads as evidence a parent approved something — which would be false, and is exactly the kind of thing that gets cited in a subject access request or a DPA review. Commented directly in `create_school_passport()` (0140) for this reason: harmless while everyone building on this schema knows why, dangerous the moment someone reads it at face value without that context. The general lesson: when a column's own name stops matching what actually causes it to be set, comment the write site, don't just remember it.

- **DEAD RLS POLICIES DON'T ANNOUNCE THEMSELVES — NAME THEM WHEN THE LAST CALLER GOES.** `passport_access`'s "Teachers can insert access for approved, matching institutions" and "Teachers can reactivate their own revoked access" (live definitions: `0100`) both gate on `passport_institution_links.approved_by_parent = true`. `AddChildSheet` was their only real caller — the principal-driven `grant_passport_access()` is `SECURITY DEFINER` and bypasses ordinary RLS on `passport_access` entirely, so it never touched these policies. Deleting `AddChildSheet` (PRD 3 Stage 2) makes both policies permanently unreachable by any real flow. Dead, not wrong — not urgent to remove, but nothing else in the schema will ever surface that fact; the next person auditing `approved_by_parent`'s remaining live dependents would otherwise have to work this out from scratch.

- **BEFORE TEARING DOWN ANY FIXTURE, GREP THE ADVERSARIAL SUITE FOR ITS INSTITUTION CODE AND ACCOUNT EMAILS.** A fixture that looks like leaked debris can still be a live dependency: `ZZFIXTURESTAGE1` was correctly identified as leaked test data and correctly torn down (`scripts/dev/teardown.mjs`) — but `CHECK W`'s own `W11c` referenced that exact institution as its one live example of a `'grandfathered'` `approval_source` value (the signature of `0101`'s one-time historical backfill, which no production path can ever reproduce), and nothing checked for that reference before deleting it. One command — `grep -rn "<institution_code>\|<email-prefix>" scripts/incident-log-test/adversarial-verify.mjs` — would have caught it before the deletion instead of after, in a full-suite run's failure list.

- **`principal_handovers` HAS NO CASCADE ON ITS `institution_staff` FOREIGN KEYS — DELIBERATELY — AND A RAW TEARDOWN DELETE WILL HIT IT.** A fixture that exercises `hand_over_principal()` (real handover records, not just the staff rows themselves) cannot delete those staff rows with a plain `institution_staff` delete afterward — `principal_handovers` (migration `0102`) references `institution_staff.id` from FOUR columns, none of them `on delete cascade`, matching this schema's own audit-trail pattern of never letting a legal/history record silently lose its referent: `principal_handovers_predecessor_institution_staff_id_fkey` (not null, always set), `principal_handovers_successor_old_institution_staff_id_fkey` (not null, always set), `principal_handovers_successor_new_institution_staff_id_fkey` (not null, always set), and `principal_handovers_predecessor_new_institution_staff_id_fkey` (nullable, set only when `outcome = 'staying'`). Hit for real tearing down PRD 5 Stage 2's handover-test fixture — confirmed live, not guessed, via the actual Postgres error on the first constraint. The fix: delete the fixture's own `principal_handovers` rows first, then the `institution_staff` rows, same ordering `scripts/dev/teardown.mjs` already uses for every other non-cascading audit table. This is the audit trail working as designed, not a bug to route around.

  **CORRECTION, SCOPED PRECISELY, 21 Sept 2026 — the entry above is real but was read as broader than it is, and nearly caused a full-database wipe script to over-engineer around a trap that doesn't apply to it.** `principal_handovers` also carries its own `institution_id` column, `on delete cascade` from `institutions` (`0102`) — so it is itself a cascade-sibling of `institution_staff` under `institutions`, not an unrelated table sitting outside that tree. Tested directly, both ways, against a real fixture before writing the regenerated wipe scripts: `delete from institutions` (cascading `institution_staff` and `principal_handovers` together, in the same statement) **succeeds cleanly** — Postgres resolves the cross-reference within the cascade closure, the same way it already does for `bank_assets`/`strategy_bank`'s own cross-reference and `institution_tags`/`episode_tags`/`clinical_lead_scope`'s `on delete restrict` one. A raw `delete from institution_staff where id in (...)`, targeting specific rows directly without going through `institutions`, **fails** with exactly the FK violation this entry describes — confirmed with the identical fixture, same session. **The general rule this narrows to: the trap fires when a script deletes `institution_staff` rows directly by id — what every fixture teardown does, since a teardown targets specific accounts, not a whole institution's cascade — not when a script deletes the `institutions` row itself and lets `institution_staff` fall out via cascade, which is what a full institution-rooted wipe does.** Read this entry as "clear `principal_handovers` before a direct `institution_staff` delete" (teardown scripts, exactly as written above) — not as "a full wipe must special-case `principal_handovers`" (it doesn't need to; it's a no-op there, safe to include anyway for defense-in-depth, per `scripts/full-database-wipe-2026-09-21.sql`'s own header).

- **FIXTURES AND NEW LIFECYCLE STATE.** When a migration adds a state a production path is responsible for setting, existing fixtures WILL break — and that is correct. Fix them by driving the real production path (the RPC, as a real signed-in session), never by setting the column directly in a service-role insert. A fixture that sets such a column directly is testing the database's shape, not the system's behaviour. This happened for real when `0100_institution_join_approval.sql` made `class_teacher`/`sna` joins pending by default: every fixture across the suite that inserted staff via service role had encoded "row exists = active," correctly went false, and 353 checks failed loudly rather than passing against a state production no longer produces — the opposite outcome to the earlier `status: "awaiting_attestation"` incident (a fixture hand-setting a column production derives, which stayed green while masking a real bug through 180 checks), from the identical root cause. The fix in both cases is the same: call the real RPC (`approve_staff_join()`, as the principal's own session), never assign the column. One more thing worth naming from this repair pass: fixing ~6 fixture blocks this way exercised `approve_staff_join()`'s happy path repeatedly as a side effect, but not one existing check asserts on that RPC's *own* correctness (authorization, refusing a non-principal or wrong-institution caller, refusing double-approval) — every downstream check only needed the state it produces to be right, not the RPC's own behavior verified. That verification doesn't exist yet anywhere in the suite; a fixture-repair pass fixing fixtures is not the same claim as Step 2 writing real coverage for a migration's own new surface, and shouldn't be mistaken for it.

- **The adversarial suite tests the database, not the journey — it never renders or drives a UI.** Every check calls the Supabase client directly with a real JWT; none of them touch a React component, a redirect, or conditional rendering. A check whose NAME describes a user-facing outcome ("a deactivated person rejoining...") can pass while the actual page a person uses is broken, if the bug lives entirely in client-side routing/gating logic rather than in RLS/an RPC/a trigger — the class of thing this suite is built to catch. This happened for real: `join-institution/page.tsx`'s `checkExisting()` had no `deactivated_at` filter, silently redirecting a deactivated person back to their old dashboard instead of the join form, while CHECK V10's own direct-INSERT check kept passing, honestly proving the database would accept a legitimate rejoin — a true claim, just narrower than the check's name promised. Found only by hand, not by the suite. When a check's claim is about what a user would *experience* rather than what the database *permits*, write it against the CLIENT'S OWN QUERY SHAPE, file:line-matched to the component that gates the real journey — not a proxy for it, the literal same filter — so a regression in that client code fails the check the same way a regression in an RLS policy would.

- **COVERAGE GAPS ARE NOT WRONG-REASON PASSES.** A wrong-reason pass is a check that asserts the wrong thing. A coverage gap is a mechanism with no check at all, exercised only as a side effect of other fixtures. `approve_staff_join()` had six fixtures depending on its happy path and zero checks on its guards. When a new RPC is added, ask what asserts its REFUSALS — not whether anything uses it.

- **THE SUITE ENCODES OUR BELIEFS, NOT TRUTH.** Adversarial checks catch contradictions between code and spec. They cannot catch an error IN the spec — a check written from a wrong assumption will confirm the bug. `0105` tightened an edit policy per instruction; the accompanying check asserted the same wrong thing and passed. When a check is written from a stated requirement rather than from observed behaviour, ask what would have to be true for the requirement itself to be wrong.

- **A CHECK CAN ENCODE A BUG AS A SPEC.** A different failure from a wrong-reason pass: a wrong-reason pass asserts the wrong thing while believing it's testing something else; this is a check that asserts EXACTLY what it claims, faithfully and correctly — and what it claims is itself wrong. CHECK C, named "SNA can stamp but cannot complete stage two or sign off," asserted that limitation as a PASS. It was telling the truth about the database's own behaviour the whole time — `owning_teacher_id = null` for an SNA-created incident, silently unwritable by anyone, forever (migration 0159's own bug). The check wasn't lying; the thing it was faithfully proving was the defect. When a check's NAME describes a limitation rather than a guarantee — "cannot", "is refused", "has no" used about a *capability* rather than an *unauthorized actor* — ask whether the limitation is the one actually intended, not just whether the check passes. A second instance found in the same pass: `claim_incident()` existed as the designed recovery path for exactly this scenario, with zero client callers, ever — an escape hatch built and never wired up, which is how the bug shipped invisibly for as long as it did.

- **A CHECK CAN BE WRONG FROM THE START AND PASS BY LUCK — A FOURTH WAY A GREEN CHECK CAN MEAN NOTHING, ALONGSIDE A WRONG-REASON PASS, A CHECK ENCODING A BUG AS A SPEC, AND A CHECK ENCODING SUPERSEDED BEHAVIOUR.** `EEE4a`/`EEE5b`/`EEE5c` resolved a `message_categories` row via `.contains("allowed_sender_roles", ["class_teacher"])` with no `applies_to` filter and no `ORDER BY`, took the first row back, and happened to get a `child`-scoped category for months — Postgres makes no row-order guarantee without one, and nothing forced the fixture to confront that. `0168`'s staff-to-staff categories (three new `applies_to: 'staff'` rows, all carrying `class_teacher` in `allowed_sender_roles`) shifted the unordered result, and the same query started returning a `staff` row first. `send_message()` correctly refused it on a child conversation; the check reported a failure that was entirely its own doing. No real caller could ever reproduce this — `useMessageCategories.ts` always passes an explicit `applies_to` and orders by `sort_order`, deterministically, every time. **An unordered query in a fixture is not a test, it's a coin toss that happened to land the same way for a while.** The other three ways a green check deceives (above: wrong-reason pass, encoding-a-bug-as-a-spec, and encoding superseded behaviour) all involve the check asserting something FALSE and getting away with it; this one is different in kind — the check was never actually pinned to the thing its name claims to prove, so its historical PASSES were never evidence of anything either, not just its one recent FAIL. Any fixture query feeding a `record()` assertion that lacks an explicit filter/order clause a real caller would always supply is a latent instance of this, waiting for an unrelated data change to flip it.

- **WHEN ACCESS OR AUTHORITY IS GRANTED, TEST THE DESTINATION.** Two gaps found in one session: an ownership transfer whose recipient could not edit what they received (`AA-8` proved the transfer record but never asked whether the new owner could act on it — `can_own_incident()` never recognised a principal as eligible), and an SNA assignment with no route for the assignee to reach the child (`assign_sna_to_child()` has been live since Stage 2; `/sna/passports` only ever queried `passport_access` directly, never `has_sna_access()` or any of the other access sources — the suite proved the grant, nothing asked whether the person could get to it). In both cases the grant was correct and adversarially proven. Granting access and being able to USE it are separate claims, and the suite only ever tests the first — it never renders a route. For every new access source, ask which screen surfaces it, and prove that screen queries the right thing.

  **Two more instances of the identical shape, found live on the deployed app during Stage 3's own browser-verification pass, in the SAME session that had just fixed `/sna/passports`** — a reminder that fixing one occurrence doesn't inoculate the neighbouring surfaces: (1) `useSnaChildren.ts`'s merged list correctly resolved a covering SNA's class-wide roster, but did it by reading `class_children` directly — a table whose own SELECT policy (0104) is deliberately narrower than `has_sna_access()`, current class teacher or principal only. The list query returned nothing, RLS-silent, for exactly the covering SNA it was built for; fixed with a narrow SECURITY DEFINER RPC (`get_temporary_access_covered_children()`), not a widening of that policy. (2) The list itself, once fixed, linked into `/sna/passport/[passportId]` — whose OWN access guard had never been touched, still a direct `passport_access`-only read predating this stage, refusing the exact child the list had just shown as accessible ("we couldn't find this classroom profile"). Fixed by replacing that guard with a call to `has_sna_access()` itself, so the guard and the underlying RLS can never independently drift again. Generalised: a fix at one layer (the list's own query) does not imply the layers it reads from or links to were fixed too — each hop in "grant → list → detail → write" is a separate claim, and needs testing separately. A THIRD component from the same stage (`TemporaryAccessBanner`) turned out to have been built, working, and never imported anywhere at all — found only by trying to see it live, not by any code-level reasoning; a component's own correctness proves nothing about whether it was ever wired in.

  **A separate, unrelated tooling trap surfaced during the same pass, worth recording so it isn't mistaken for a real bug again**: Next.js's client-side Router Cache can keep serving a stale RSC payload for a route across repeated `location.href` reassignments and even explicit hard reloads WITHIN THE SAME BROWSER TAB, even after the underlying deploy has genuinely gone green (confirmed via response headers showing `x-vercel-cache: MISS`, and the served JS bundle itself containing the new code, searched for directly). It looked exactly like the fix hadn't shipped. Closing the tab and opening a fresh one cleared it. If a just-deployed fix doesn't seem to be taking effect in browser verification despite the deploy SHA checking out, try a genuinely fresh tab before concluding the fix is wrong.

- **QUERY LIVE STATE, NOT AN EVENT LOG, FOR ANYTHING ACTIONABLE.** `school_notices` is append-only with nothing keeping it in sync with the tables holding current state. `'attestation_withdrawn'` fires once and knows nothing about a later re-attestation; `'incident_parent_call'` knows nothing about `mark_parent_called()`. A dashboard built on notices shows resolved items as outstanding until someone clicks them away — a persistent flag standing in for a live fact. Notices are a record of what happened; they are not a source of what is currently true. One extra join, and it cannot drift. Decided during PRD 2 Stage 7's own dashboard recon, weighing `school_notices` against querying `incident_children`/`incident_attestations` directly for "restraints needing a parent call" and "withdrawn attestations institution-wide" — neither built on notices, both on live state.

- **A PRINCIPAL MAY SEE AND SORT BY WHO LOGGED AN INCIDENT — THE PRD 4 STAGE 3 RULE AGAINST THIS WAS WRONG, OVERRULED.** `principal/incidents/page.tsx` originally shipped with "no Reporter/author column and no sortable 'who logged this' axis," reasoning that staff should appear as authors within a record (the countersign report), never as a column to sort by. Overruled by Daniel directly: a principal seeing one teacher's name recur across incidents is identifying where support is needed, not ranking people — that's the product's actual purpose, and withholding it made the tool worse at its own job. The distinction that matters, and the one to preserve going forward: identification and sorting are fine; what must never be built is anything that presents this as a performance metric — a league table, a score, a ranking framed as evaluation. A sortable column naming who logged an incident is fine. A count, a leaderboard, or copy implying comparison between staff is not, and needs its own conversation before it gets built, not a silent extension of this decision.

- **THE SUPPORT BUTTON IS THE ONE DELIBERATE RED IN THIS PRODUCT.** The nav icon and the raised alert are red because a teacher presses it when a situation is out of hand and they cannot manage alone. Golden Brown — the app's attention colour — reads as an outstanding task, which this is not. It must NOT use the Calm pair, which signals a distressed child rather than a call for adults. Its own token, used nowhere else (`--color-support-red-raw` / `--color-brand-support-red`, `#D7263D`, `src/app/globals.css`). The no-semantic-red rule still holds everywhere else.

- **A DELIBERATE BREAK IN VISUAL CONTINUITY IS NOT THE SAME AS A BROKEN PAGE — THE TEST IS WHETHER CONTENT AND STYLE CHANGE TOGETHER.** The consent/agreement screens (`src/components/consent/`) were rebuilt medtech-sharp — Inter, near-monochrome, hairline rules, a content-width pill button — deliberately unlike the rest of the app's warm, rounded, Baloo-2-and-card language, including dropping the brand icon entirely (kept only a small grey text wordmark) once it turned out that icon itself contains Golden Brown fills that would have broken the screens' own stricter colour rule. Checked live, arriving from the login screen: it reads as a different kind of moment, not a different (or broken) app. The reason, worth reusing whenever a screen is deliberately styled off-system: a broken or half-loaded page is FAMILIAR content in the WRONG style (a login form in the wrong font); this is UNMISTAKABLY NEW content — legal-feeling ticks and "BEFORE YOU START" rather than an email/password field — arriving in a new style, AT THE SAME MOMENT. Content and style shifting together reads as intentional; style shifting alone, or shifting only partway (some elements sharp, others still rounded), is what would have read as a bug. The class-management redesign will face the identical question — apply this same test, not a fresh gut check: does the new style arrive WITH new content, or is it grafted onto the same content the rest of the app already shows the same way.

- **"OWNERSHIP IS PERMANENT" MEANS THE RECORD SURVIVES AND THE AUTHOR STAYS ATTRIBUTED — IT NEVER MEANT A PERSONAL READ KEY THAT OUTLIVES THE RELATIONSHIP. `session_notes` AND `assessments` BOTH GOT THIS WRONG BY DEFAULT, FIXED IN 0233.** Both tables were built with a straightforward author-permanent SELECT policy — a clinician reads their own material regardless of anything else, because "ownership is permanent" was the standing precedent (`session_notes`, PRD 6 Stage 1). Neither table's own build ever actually asked "what happens after this client is discharged" — the question was never raised, not answered wrong. PRD 7 Stage 2's own discharge-case verification asked it directly, empirically, and found `assessments` (and `attachments`, by inheritance through the storage bridge) still readable by a practitioner whose caseload access to that client had already ended.

  **Daniel's decision: access follows the live relationship, not authorship.** The practitioner reads while `clinician_access.is_active` is true; the director reads regardless, permanently, on the strength of their own organisational standing rather than a personal relationship to the client at all. **The precedent this is not a departure from, named explicitly so nobody reads it as a reversal**: `_close_child_access_for_enrolment_end()` (0121/0123) — when a child's school enrolment ends, a class teacher's own ACCESS closes; the incident record they wrote stays exactly as it was, their own name stays on it, and they simply can no longer read it. Their principal still can. A clinic discharge is the identical event with different words — a live relationship ending, not a record being taken away. "Ownership is permanent" and "access follows the live relationship" were never in tension; the mistake was applying only the first half of that pair to two tables and never asking about the second.

  **Scope checked BEFORE building, not assumed** — Daniel's own explicit instruction: does anything else in the clinician track share the author-permanent shape? Checked directly against the LIVE migration text for `abc_logs`, `fba_reports`, `fba_afls_data`/`afls_assessments`, and `fba_instrument_requests` — all four already require `clinician_access.is_active = true` on every clinician-facing policy, confirmed by reading the policy text, not assumed from the table name. The author-permanent gap was confined to exactly two tables (`session_notes`, `assessments`) plus one inheriting it (`attachments`) — three tables fixed by 0233, not six.

  **The routing-inconsistency finding this same verification surfaced was never a database bug** — the Clinical File page refusing a discharged client while the assessment's own direct URL didn't was two DIFFERENT gates disagreeing (the Clinical File page's own pre-existing, unrelated live-caseload-standing check vs. `assessments`' own author-permanent RLS), not one gate with a hole in it. Fixing `assessments`' own RLS to match the same live-standing rule made both routes converge on the same answer for the same reason they used to disagree — no client-side gate needed touching. Proven directly, live, browser, same PA session, same discharged client: the Clinical File page (`/clinician/passport/[id]`) says "We couldn't find this passport, or you don't have access to it," and the direct assessment URL (`/clinician/passport/[id]/assessment/[id]`) says "This assessment couldn't be found" — both refuse, where before only one did.

- **A SECURITY DEFINER HELPER THAT RE-QUERIES ITS OWN TABLE BY ID BREAKS THE MOMENT A CLIENT CHAINS `.insert().select()` AGAINST THAT SAME TABLE.** Found live, by the very first run of 0233's own verification fixture, before any real clinician was exposed to it — not by review. `_assessment_is_readable_by_caller(p_assessment_id uuid)` (0233's first draft) re-queried `public.assessments` by id from inside a SECURITY DEFINER function called by `assessments`' own SELECT policy. When Postgres evaluates the RETURNING clause of an `INSERT INTO assessments ...` statement, that policy check runs as part of the SAME statement — and the helper's own self-referential re-query of `assessments` does not see the row currently being inserted, so the whole call is refused with a bare "new row violates row-level security policy," even though (a) a bare insert with no chained select succeeds every time, and (b) the identical predicate, called a moment later as a genuinely separate statement against the same now-committed row, correctly returns true. This is exactly the shape of `NewAssessmentSheet.tsx`'s own real, production create-assessment call — `.insert({...}).select("id").single()` — meaning the bug, while 0233 was live, broke assessment creation for every clinician, every time.

  **Why `session_notes`' own identically-shaped policy never had this problem**: it's a flat inline expression referencing the row's own columns directly (`clinician_id = auth.uid() and ...`) — no subquery against `session_notes` itself at all. `assessments`' own policy, wrapped in a single helper taking only the assessment's `id`, had to re-derive `clinician_id`/`passport_id` by querying `assessments` again to get them — that re-query is the one structural difference that introduced the self-reference.

  **The fix (0234): make the helper a pure predicate over the row's own column VALUES, passed in directly — `_assessment_is_readable_by_caller(p_clinician_id uuid, p_passport_id uuid)` — never re-querying the table it protects.** `assessments`' own USING clause now reads `_assessment_is_readable_by_caller(clinician_id, passport_id)`, matching `session_notes`' own flat shape exactly. The attachments bridge's own use of the same predicate (`_caller_owns_artefact`'s 'assessment' arm) is NOT self-referential — it's evaluated from `attachments`'/`storage.objects`' own policies, never from within an `INSERT INTO assessments` statement — so its own cross-table lookup of `assessments` by id (to resolve `clinician_id`/`passport_id` before delegating) stays exactly as it was, just repointed at the new two-argument signature.

  **The general rule: a SECURITY DEFINER helper that a table's own RLS policy calls must never re-query that SAME table by id.** If the policy needs values from the row being checked, take them as parameters computed from columns already in scope in the USING/WITH CHECK expression (as `session_notes` always did), not as a fresh lookup inside the helper. This is a narrower, RLS-specific instance of a wider Postgres truth — a statement's own RETURNING clause does not see itself through an independent subquery — but it wasn't obvious from reading either version of the function, and it will not be obvious to the next person who reaches for "wrap the whole check in one helper taking just the id" as the cleaner-looking refactor.

  **A second, smaller mistake made and caught in the same fix, worth naming because it's this schema's own already-documented trap, hit again**: the first version of 0234 tried `DROP FUNCTION` on the old one-argument signature BEFORE repointing the policy to the new two-argument one — refused outright by Postgres (`2BP01`, "other objects depend on it"), because the policy was still a live, tracked dependency on the old signature. Fixed by reordering: `ALTER POLICY` to repoint to the new function FIRST, then `DROP FUNCTION` on the now-unreferenced old one. Exactly migration 0226's own already-documented ordering rule for this trap, made again in a fresh migration despite the precedent already being written down — a reminder that "we have a rule for this" doesn't prevent the mistake unless the rule is actually checked against before writing the DROP statement, not just known to exist somewhere in this file.

- **A COLUMN `DEFAULT` CAN NEVER CONTAIN A SUBQUERY, EVEN A SCALAR ONE WRAPPING A SINGLE FUNCTION CALL.** `alter table fba_reports add column domain_tags ... default (select ... from some_function(...) r)` (migration 0235's own first attempt) is illegal Postgres syntax — `ERROR: 0A000: cannot use subquery in DEFAULT expression` — not a logic bug, a hard syntax restriction with no workaround at the DEFAULT-clause level. The fix: wrap the lookup in a small scalar-returning helper function and call THAT directly as the default (`default public._fba_reports_default_domain_tags()`) — a DEFAULT may call a function freely, even one whose own body contains a subquery; the restriction is on the clause's own syntax, never on what a function it calls is permitted to do internally. Confirmed live: the Supabase SQL editor does not wrap a pasted script in one transaction (matching the earlier 0234 dependency-ordering incident) — everything in 0235 before this statement had already committed by the time it errored, so the corrective migration (0236) was written fully idempotent throughout (a `DO` block guard for `CREATE TYPE`, since Postgres has no `CREATE TYPE IF NOT EXISTS`; `IF NOT EXISTS` on every `ADD COLUMN`; drop-then-create for the one `CREATE POLICY`; `ON CONFLICT DO NOTHING` on the seed rows) so it was safe to run regardless of exactly where 0235 had stopped, rather than requiring anyone to first work out how far a partial run got.

- **PERMISSIVE RLS POLICIES COMBINE WITH OR — FIXING ONE SELECT POLICY DOES NOT CLOSE A GATE IF ANY OTHER POLICY ON THE SAME TABLE ALSO GRANTS SELECT, INCLUDING A `for all` MANAGEMENT POLICY THAT LOOKS UNRELATED. THE OPPOSITE OF "A REBUILD CAN SILENTLY DROP A GATE" (ABOVE) — NOT A GATE DROPPED, A GATE HELD OPEN. Found live, Client Info build, 22 Sept 2026.** Migration 0287's own item 6 fixed `session_types`' plain "Institution staff can view..." SELECT policy to require `inst.type = 'clinic'`, closing the exact gap this file already documents for `strategy_bank`/`bank_assets` (the "WERE WRITABLE BY A SCHOOL" entry, above — the same table, an earlier stage of the identical mistake). Proven with the sharp adversarial test that entry's own fix established: a fake row inserted for a real school's own `institution_id` (service role, bypassing the app), read attempted through that school's own real principal session. `strategy_bank` refused cleanly. `session_types` did not — the same school principal read the fake row straight back.

  **Why: `session_types` has a SECOND policy that independently governs SELECT, and it was never touched.** `"Directors can manage their own institution's session types"` (0281) is declared `for all`, not `for select`/`for insert`/`for update` separately — and Postgres OR-combines every permissive policy that covers a given command, so a `for all` policy's own `USING` clause is just as much a SELECT policy as the one explicitly named for reading. Fixing the SELECT-specific policy alone left this second, ungated one standing, and a principal reading through it never touched the fixed policy at all. `strategy_bank` never had this shape — its own write policies are INSERT-only and UPDATE-only, two separate single-command policies, no `for all` — so its SELECT-specific policy was the ONLY thing governing SELECT there, and the original fix was already complete. Fixed in 0288: the same `inst.type = 'clinic'` clause added to the `for all` policy's own `USING` and `WITH CHECK`, re-proven with the identical fake-row test until it refused too.

  **The general rule this earns: when tightening read access on any table, list EVERY policy on that table first, not just the one named for reading, and check each one's own effect on SELECT specifically.** A `for all` policy, a policy with no command suffix in its own name, or any policy whose name describes writing ("can manage", "can curate", "can edit") can still be a live SELECT grant if its own command clause is `for all` rather than a narrower one — the policy's NAME is not evidence of which commands it actually covers; only reading its own `for <command>` clause is. This is the mirror image of the "a rebuild can silently drop a gate" family already documented above (a migration that REMOVES a check nobody meant to remove) — here nothing was removed at all; the original gap simply had two independent doors, one was locked, and the other was never even inventoried. Whenever a table's read access is being tightened, the correct first step is `grep` for every `create policy ... on public.<table>` block touching that table, across every migration, and read each one's own command clause before declaring the read boundary closed.

  **A second, related finding from the same session, worth recording alongside it: the client_contact_info/client_clinical_intake tables (0287/0288) had every guarantee they claim — the admin exclusion, the school exclusion, the parent split, the suspected_diagnosis isolation, the clinic_admin-at-a-clinic-only claim-code widening — proven only by a one-off verification script, never added to the standing suite. The suite's own count (1199) was identical before and after the whole feature shipped, which is itself the tell: a real guarantee with no standing regression coverage is one migration away from silently breaking with nothing to catch it. Closed in the same pass as this entry — CHECK VVV (own institution pair, own fixture, own teardown) now covers all five of Daniel's own named cases, including this exact policy-combination shape as its own sharp test (VVV-D).**

# Frontend layout gotchas

- **A CLASS THAT LOOKS RIGHT AND DOES NOTHING IS INVISIBLE TO A GREP-BASED SWEEP. MEASURE WITH `getBoundingClientRect()`, DON'T GREP FOR CLASSES.** Two prior desktop-width sweeps on the principal track both missed real stretched elements. The reason: `w-full lg:w-auto` — the correct, established pattern for "full width on mobile, content-width on desktop" — was already present on ten buttons/links and doing nothing on two of them. A grep for the class found it and called it fixed; nobody had actually measured the rendered width.
